Yeah, um… what made me finally call the EAP is I’ve been getting really jumpy at work. Like, every little bang in the office—somebody slams a file drawer, or drops a coffee mug—and I jump like it’s this huge thing.  

And then my chest keeps racing way longer than it should. It’s embarrassing, honestly. The office isn’t even that loud most of the time, except people are always doing that thing where they yank drawers shut like they’re trying to prove a point… but yeah, that’s the main reason I came in.

The office isn’t even that loud most of the time, except people are always doing that thing where they yank drawers shut like they’re trying to prove a point… but yeah, that’s the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel jumpy or experience a racing chest in situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, yeah. Like the file drawers, or someone drops a mug, and I jump way harder than I should, then my chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, I guess I’m not around as many sudden bangs, unless you count my neighbor upstairs who walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight—but I don’t know, the thing that really made me call was the office stuff. It just feels ridiculous to be startled by a drawer, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not around any loud noises or sudden movements?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Even when it’s not loud, like when I’m just sitting at my desk, I catch myself checking where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s walking up behind me. Which is stupid because I’m supposed to be watching my team and answering emails and all that.\n\nIt’s not like the office is some dangerous place—it’s mostly people complaining about spreadsheets and the break room coffee—but my attention keeps going there anyway, like I need to know where everybody is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious at home or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid as much attention to it there. I’m usually just trying to make dinner or crash on the couch, and then I get distracted by dumb stuff like whether I remembered to take the laundry out—my building’s machines are always full, so if you miss the timing, someone moves your clothes, which is… annoying.\n\nBut anxiety-wise, alone? I guess I’m not sure how to answer that. It’s not as obvious to me as it is at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around other people outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, outside of work I’m usually around people in pretty normal ways—grocery store, maybe grabbing food, my friend had this barbecue thing last weekend but I got there late because parking near his place is a nightmare, there’s this one street where everyone double-parks and then you’re stuck doing a three-point turn forever…\n\nSorry, what were you asking—around other people? I don’t think I’ve noticed anything clear like that. Not in the same way, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet place by yourself?\n\nPatient: Quiet by myself… I don’t know. I mean, quiet is kind of rare, honestly—my apartment has this radiator that clicks even when it’s not doing anything, and the guy upstairs walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight.\n\nBut if you mean like actually alone with no noise… I haven’t really noticed. Sometimes I just put on a show or scroll through stuff, so I’m not sitting there thinking about it. I don’t think I can give you a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're about to go to work or anticipating a workday?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, not always on the way there exactly—like if I’m stuck in traffic I’m mostly mad about traffic, and there’s this one light that takes forever near the office park.\n\nBut once I’m thinking about being in the office, or when I get there, I catch myself… checking stuff. Like where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s coming up behind me. Even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team, I’m sort of scanning the room instead. It’s hard to just focus like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your work or job that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think it’s tied to that thing with my boss. Or—well, not even the job itself, because the spreadsheets and calls are just normal boring stuff, and I used to complain more about the coffee machine than anything else.\n\nBut when I try to explain what happened, I can remember him slamming the chair and standing in front of the door… like, I can picture that really clearly. But the worst part, the actual words he said to me, it’s just blank. Like that section got cut out. And then being back there makes me feel like I’m trying to fill in something I can’t quite get to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure. I mean, I guess I didn’t feel like I was in charge of the situation, if that’s what you mean. But “powerless” feels like a big word.

So maybe I felt trapped more than anything. I don’t know. I mean, outside of work… I haven’t really sat down and made a list of when it comes up, you know? It’s not like I’m at the grocery store thinking, “Oh, there’s my boss again,” or something.

It’s embarrassing, honestly. The office isn’t even that loud most of the time, except people are always doing that thing where they yank drawers shut like they’re trying to prove a point… but yeah, that’s the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel jumpy or experience a racing chest in situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, yeah. Like the file drawers, or someone drops a mug, and I jump way harder than I should, then my chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, I guess I’m not around as many sudden bangs, unless you count my neighbor upstairs who walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight—but I don’t know, the thing that really made me call was the office stuff. It just feels ridiculous to be startled by a drawer, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not around any loud noises or sudden movements?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Even when it’s not loud, like when I’m just sitting at my desk, I catch myself checking where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s walking up behind me. Which is stupid because I’m supposed to be watching my team and answering emails and all that.\n\nIt’s not like the office is some dangerous place—it’s mostly people complaining about spreadsheets and the break room coffee—but my attention keeps going there anyway, like I need to know where everybody is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious at home or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid as much attention to it there. I’m usually just trying to make dinner or crash on the couch, and then I get distracted by dumb stuff like whether I remembered to take the laundry out—my building’s machines are always full, so if you miss the timing, someone moves your clothes, which is… annoying.\n\nBut anxiety-wise, alone? I guess I’m not sure how to answer that. It’s not as obvious to me as it is at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around other people outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, outside of work I’m usually around people in pretty normal ways—grocery store, maybe grabbing food, my friend had this barbecue thing last weekend but I got there late because parking near his place is a nightmare, there’s this one street where everyone double-parks and then you’re stuck doing a three-point turn forever…\n\nSorry, what were you asking—around other people? I don’t think I’ve noticed anything clear like that. Not in the same way, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet place by yourself?\n\nPatient: Quiet by myself… I don’t know. I mean, quiet is kind of rare, honestly—my apartment has this radiator that clicks even when it’s not doing anything, and the guy upstairs walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight.\n\nBut if you mean like actually alone with no noise… I haven’t really noticed. Sometimes I just put on a show or scroll through stuff, so I’m not sitting there thinking about it. I don’t think I can give you a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're about to go to work or anticipating a workday?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, not always on the way there exactly—like if I’m stuck in traffic I’m mostly mad about traffic, and there’s this one light that takes forever near the office park.\n\nBut once I’m thinking about being in the office, or when I get there, I catch myself… checking stuff. Like where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s coming up behind me. Even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team, I’m sort of scanning the room instead. I can’t get the words. It’s weird, because I remember other random stuff from that day, like the carpet pattern and the buzzing light, but the actual part that upset me most is just missing, like somebody cut that section out.\n\nDoctor: Did your boss say or do something that felt threatening or traumatic to you during that incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. The part I can actually remember is him slamming the chair, really hard, and then standing in front of the door like I couldn’t get past him.\n\nBut the actual worst part—what he said to me—I can’t… I don’t have it. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what made me finally call the EAP is I’ve been getting really jumpy at work. Like, every little bang in the office—somebody slams a file drawer, or drops a coffee mug—and I jump like it’s this huge thing.  \n\nAnd then my chest keeps racing way longer than it should. It’s embarrassing, honestly. The office isn’t even that loud most of the time, except people are always doing that thing where they yank drawers shut like they’re trying to prove a point… but yeah, that’s the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel jumpy or experience a racing chest in situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, yeah. Like the file drawers, or someone drops a mug, and I jump way harder than I should, then my chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, I guess I’m not around as many sudden bangs, unless you count my neighbor upstairs who walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight—but I don’t know, the thing that really made me call was the office stuff. It just feels ridiculous to be startled by a drawer, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not around any loud noises or sudden movements?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Even when it’s not loud, like when I’m just sitting at my desk, I catch myself checking where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s walking up behind me. Which is stupid because I’m supposed to be watching my team and answering emails and all that.\n\nIt’s not like the office is some dangerous place—it’s mostly people complaining about spreadsheets and the break room coffee—but my attention keeps going there anyway, like I need to know where everybody is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious at home or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid as much attention to it there. I’m usually just trying to make dinner or crash on the couch, and then I get distracted by dumb stuff like whether I remembered to take the laundry out—my building’s machines are always full, so if you miss the timing, someone moves your clothes, which is… annoying.\n\nBut anxiety-wise, alone? I guess I’m not sure how to answer that. It’s not as obvious to me as it is at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around other people outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, outside of work I’m usually around people in pretty normal ways—grocery store, maybe grabbing food, my friend had this barbecue thing last weekend but I got there late because parking near his place is a nightmare, there’s this one street where everyone double-parks and then you’re stuck doing a three-point turn forever…\n\nSorry, what were you asking—around other people? I don’t think I’ve noticed anything clear like that. Not in the same way, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet place by yourself?\n\nPatient: Quiet by myself… I don’t know. I mean, quiet is kind of rare, honestly—my apartment has this radiator that clicks even when it’s not doing anything, and the guy upstairs walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight.\n\nBut if you mean like actually alone with no noise… I haven’t really noticed. Sometimes I just put on a show or scroll through stuff, so I’m not sitting there thinking about it. I don’t think I can give you a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're about to go to work or anticipating a workday?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, not always on the way there exactly—like if I’m stuck in traffic I’m mostly mad about traffic, and there’s this one light that takes forever near the office park.\n\nBut once I’m thinking about being in the office, or when I get there, I catch myself… checking stuff. Like where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s coming up behind me. Even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team, I’m sort of scanning the room instead. It’s hard to just focus like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your work or job that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I can’t get the words. The part I can actually remember is him slamming the chair, really hard, and then standing in front of the door like I couldn’t get past him.\n\nBut the actual worst part—what he said to me—I can’t… I don’t have it. It’s like when you’re trying to remember a song lyric and you get every dumb little thing around it instead. I remember the room and the chair and where he was standing, but the words are just blank. Like that section got cut out.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were in danger during the incident with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “danger” is the right word. I just remember the chair thing and him being by the door, and my brain kind of went… like, okay, where do I stand, what do I do with my hands, can I get out without making it worse.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I keep comparing it to when someone gets too close in an elevator and you suddenly notice every floor number. And he was standing in front of the door, like right there, so I couldn’t just leave.\n\nBut when I try to say the worst part out loud, like what he actually said to me, it’s… blank. I can’t get the words. It’s weird, because I remember other random stuff from that day, like the carpet pattern and the buzzing light, but the actual part that upset me most is just missing, like somebody cut that section out.\n\nDoctor: Did your boss say or do something that felt threatening or traumatic to you during that incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. The part I can actually remember is him slamming the chair, really hard, and then standing in front of the door like I couldn’t get past him.\n\nBut the actual worst part—what he said to me—I can’t… I don’t have it. It’s like when you’re trying to remember a song lyric and you get every dumb little thing around it instead. I remember the room and the chair and where he was standing, but the words are just blank. Like that section got cut out.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were in danger during the incident with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “danger” is the right word. I mean, I wasn’t thinking in a neat sentence like that. I just remember the chair thing and him being by the door, and my brain kind of went… like, okay, where do I stand, what do I do with my hands, can I get out without making it worse.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I keep comparing it to when someone gets too close in an elevator and you suddenly notice every floor number. So maybe I felt trapped more than anything. I’m not totally sure how to answer it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the incident with your boss was something that you couldn't control or that you were powerless to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess I didn’t feel like I was in charge of the situation, if that’s what you mean. But “powerless” feels like a big word.\n\nI kept thinking about small stuff, like where to put my eyes, whether moving toward the door would make him more angry. It’s strange, because I manage people at work too, and I’m used to handling tense conversations—like, I’ve had sales guys get really heated over quotas and I can usually talk them down. But in that moment it wasn’t like that. I wasn’t really choosing what happened next, I was just kind of… waiting for it to be over, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the incident with your boss is something that still bothers you or haunts you when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, outside of work… I haven’t really sat down and made a list of when it comes up, you know? It’s not like I’m at the grocery store thinking, “Oh, there’s my boss again,” or something.\n\nAlthough grocery stores are weirdly stressful anyway—like the self-checkout yelling at you to put the item in the bagging area when it’s already there. But, sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure if I’d say it “haunts” me. That sounds kind of dramatic. It bothered me, yeah, but I don’t know how much it’s following me around when I’m not thinking about work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares or flashbacks about what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them flashbacks. Like in movies where someone is suddenly back there? I don’t think it’s like that.\n\nNightmares… I mean, I remember waking up tense a couple times, but I couldn’t tell you what I was dreaming about. Sometimes I have weird dreams anyway, like being late for a shift at a job I haven’t had since college, or my teeth falling out—apparently everyone has that one. So I don’t really know if it’s connected to him.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed clear nightmares or flashbacks about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding people, places, or things that remind you of what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way, like avoiding “people, places, or things.” I mean, I avoid the break room sometimes, but that might just be because someone keeps microwaving fish in there, which should honestly be illegal in an office.\n\nBut as far as things that remind me of him or what happened… I’m not sure. Like the file drawers, or someone drops a mug, and I jump way harder than I should, then my chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, I guess I’m not around as many sudden bangs, unless you count my neighbor upstairs who walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight—but I don’t know, the thing that really made me call was the office stuff. It just feels ridiculous to be startled by a drawer, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not around any loud noises or sudden movements?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Even when it’s not loud, like when I’m just sitting at my desk, I catch myself checking where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s walking up behind me. Which is stupid because I’m supposed to be watching my team and answering emails and all that.\n\nIt’s not like the office is some dangerous place—it’s mostly people complaining about spreadsheets and the break room coffee—but my attention keeps going there anyway, like I need to know where everybody is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious at home or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid as much attention to it there. I’m usually just trying to make dinner or crash on the couch, and then I get distracted by dumb stuff like whether I remembered to take the laundry out—my building’s machines are always full, so if you miss the timing, someone moves your clothes, which is… annoying.\n\nBut anxiety-wise, alone? I guess I’m not sure how to answer that. It’s not as obvious to me as it is at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around other people outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, outside of work I’m usually around people in pretty normal ways—grocery store, maybe grabbing food, my friend had this barbecue thing last weekend but I got there late because parking near his place is a nightmare, there’s this one street where everyone double-parks and then you’re stuck doing a three-point turn forever…\n\nSorry, what were you asking—around other people? I don’t think I’ve noticed anything clear like that. Not in the same way, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet place by yourself?\n\nPatient: Quiet by myself… I don’t know. I mean, quiet is kind of rare, honestly—my apartment has this radiator that clicks even when it’s not doing anything, and the guy upstairs walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight.\n\nBut if you mean like actually alone with no noise… I haven’t really noticed. Sometimes I just put on a show or scroll through stuff, so I’m not sitting there thinking about it. I don’t think I can give you a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're about to go to work or anticipating a workday?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, not always on the way there exactly—like if I’m stuck in traffic I’m mostly mad about traffic, and there’s this one light that takes forever near the office park.\n\nBut once I’m thinking about being in the office, or when I get there, I catch myself… checking stuff. Like where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s coming up behind me. Even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team, I’m sort of scanning the room instead. It’s hard to just focus like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your work or job that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think it’s tied to that thing with my boss. Or—well, not even the job itself, because the spreadsheets and calls are just normal boring stuff, and I used to complain more about the coffee machine than anything else.\n\nBut when I try to explain what happened, I can remember him slamming the chair and standing in front of the door… like, I can picture that really clearly. But the worst part, the actual words he said to me, it’s just blank. Like that section got cut out. And then being back there makes me feel like I’m trying to fill in something I can’t quite get to.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific and upsetting happen with your boss that might be connected to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… something happened. It was with my boss, in his office—or near his office, I guess. I keep thinking about this stupid detail, like the chair, because I remember him slamming it really hard. And he was standing in front of the door, like right there, so I couldn’t just leave.\n\nBut when I try to say the worst part out loud, like what he actually said to me, it’s… blank. I can’t get the words. It’s weird, because I remember other random stuff from that day, like the carpet pattern and the buzzing light, but the actual part that upset me most is just missing, like somebody cut that section out.\n\nDoctor: Did your boss say or do something that felt threatening or traumatic to you during that incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. The part I can actually remember is him slamming the chair, really hard, and then standing in front of the door like I couldn’t get past him.\n\nBut the actual worst part—what he said to me—I can’t… I don’t have it. It’s like when you’re trying to remember a song lyric and you get every dumb little thing around it instead. I remember the room and the chair and where he was standing, but the words are just blank. Like that section got cut out.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were in danger during the incident with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “danger” is the right word. I mean, I wasn’t thinking in a neat sentence like that. I just remember the chair thing and him being by the door, and my brain kind of went… like, okay, where do I stand, what do I do with my hands, can I get out without making it worse.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I keep comparing it to when someone gets too close in an elevator and you suddenly notice every floor number. So maybe I felt trapped more than anything. I’m not totally sure how to answer it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the incident with your boss was something that you couldn't control or that you were powerless to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess I didn’t feel like I was in charge of the situation, if that’s what you mean. But “powerless” feels like a big word.\n\nI kept thinking about small stuff, like where to put my eyes, whether moving toward the door would make him more angry. It’s strange, because I manage people at work too, and I’m used to handling tense conversations—like, I’ve had sales guys get really heated over quotas and I can usually talk them down. But in that moment it wasn’t like that. I wasn’t really choosing what happened next, I was just kind of… waiting for it to be over, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the incident with your boss is something that still bothers you or haunts you when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, outside of work… I haven’t really sat down and made a list of when it comes up, you know? It’s not like I’m at the grocery store thinking, “Oh, there’s my boss again,” or something.\n\nAlthough grocery stores are weirdly stressful anyway—like the self-checkout yelling at you to put the item in the bagging area when it’s already there. But, sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure if I’d say it “haunts” me. That sounds kind of dramatic. It bothered me, yeah, but I don’t know how much it’s following me around when I’m not thinking about work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares or flashbacks about what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them flashbacks. Like in movies where someone is suddenly back there? I don’t think it’s like that.\n\nNightmares… I mean, I remember waking up tense a couple times, but I couldn’t tell you what I was dreaming about. Sometimes I have weird dreams anyway, like being late for a shift at a job I haven’t had since college, or my teeth falling out—apparently everyone has that one. So I don’t really know if it’s connected to him.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed clear nightmares or flashbacks about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding people, places, or things that remind you of what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way, like avoiding “people, places, or things.” I mean, I avoid the break room sometimes, but that might just be because someone keeps microwaving fish in there, which should honestly be illegal in an office.\n\nBut as far as things that remind me of him or what happened… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m deliberately dodging stuff, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding your boss or situations where you might interact with him?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding him exactly. I mean, I’m not like hiding behind plants when he walks by—though our office plants are mostly fake, so that wouldn’t even work.\n\nI guess if I know he’s going to be in a meeting, I might feel a little… I don’t know, aware of it? But I can’t really say I’ve been planning my day around not seeing him or anything. I still do what I have to do. It’s just awkward, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Which is stupid because I’m supposed to be watching my team and answering emails and all that.\n\nIt’s not like the office is some dangerous place—it’s mostly people complaining about spreadsheets and the break room coffee—but my attention keeps going there anyway, like I need to know where everybody is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious at home or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid as much attention to it there. I’m usually just trying to make dinner or crash on the couch, and then I get distracted by dumb stuff like whether I remembered to take the laundry out—my building’s machines are always full, so if you miss the timing, someone moves your clothes, which is… annoying.\n\nBut anxiety-wise, alone? I guess I’m not sure how to answer that. It’s not as obvious to me as it is at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around other people outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, outside of work I’m usually around people in pretty normal ways—grocery store, maybe grabbing food, my friend had this barbecue thing last weekend but I got there late because parking near his place is a nightmare, there’s this one street where everyone double-parks and then you’re stuck doing a three-point turn forever…\n\nSorry, what were you asking—around other people? I don’t think I’ve noticed anything clear like that. Not in the same way, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet place by yourself?\n\nPatient: Quiet by myself… I don’t know. I mean, quiet is kind of rare, honestly—my apartment has this radiator that clicks even when it’s not doing anything, and the guy upstairs walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight.\n\nBut if you mean like actually alone with no noise… I haven’t really noticed. Sometimes I just put on a show or scroll through stuff, so I’m not sitting there thinking about it. I don’t think I can give you a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're about to go to work or anticipating a workday?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, not always on the way there exactly—like if I’m stuck in traffic I’m mostly mad about traffic, and there’s this one light that takes forever near the office park.\n\nBut once I’m thinking about being in the office, or when I get there, I catch myself… checking stuff. Like where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s coming up behind me. Even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team, I’m sort of scanning the room instead. It’s hard to just focus like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your work or job that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think it’s tied to that thing with my boss. Or—well, not even the job itself, because the spreadsheets and calls are just normal boring stuff, and I used to complain more about the coffee machine than anything else.\n\nBut when I try to explain what happened, I can remember him slamming the chair and standing in front of the door… like, I can picture that really clearly. But the worst part, the actual words he said to me, it’s just blank. Like that section got cut out. And then being back there makes me feel like I’m trying to fill in something I can’t quite get to.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific and upsetting happen with your boss that might be connected to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… something happened. It was with my boss, in his office—or near his office, I guess. I keep thinking about this stupid detail, like the chair, because I remember him slamming it really hard. And he was standing in front of the door, like right there, so I couldn’t just leave.\n\nBut when I try to say the worst part out loud, like what he actually said to me, it’s… blank. I can’t get the words. It’s weird, because I remember other random stuff from that day, like the carpet pattern and the buzzing light, but the actual part that upset me most is just missing, like somebody cut that section out.\n\nDoctor: Did your boss say or do something that felt threatening or traumatic to you during that incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. The part I can actually remember is him slamming the chair, really hard, and then standing in front of the door like I couldn’t get past him.\n\nBut the actual worst part—what he said to me—I can’t… I don’t have it. It’s like when you’re trying to remember a song lyric and you get every dumb little thing around it instead. I remember the room and the chair and where he was standing, but the words are just blank. Like that section got cut out.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were in danger during the incident with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “danger” is the right word. I mean, I wasn’t thinking in a neat sentence like that. I just remember the chair thing and him being by the door, and my brain kind of went… like, okay, where do I stand, what do I do with my hands, can I get out without making it worse.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I keep comparing it to when someone gets too close in an elevator and you suddenly notice every floor number. So maybe I felt trapped more than anything. I’m not totally sure how to answer it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the incident with your boss was something that you couldn't control or that you were powerless to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess I didn’t feel like I was in charge of the situation, if that’s what you mean. But “powerless” feels like a big word.\n\nI kept thinking about small stuff, like where to put my eyes, whether moving toward the door would make him more angry. It’s strange, because I manage people at work too, and I’m used to handling tense conversations—like, I’ve had sales guys get really heated over quotas and I can usually talk them down. But in that moment it wasn’t like that. I wasn’t really choosing what happened next, I was just kind of… waiting for it to be over, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the incident with your boss is something that still bothers you or haunts you when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, outside of work… I haven’t really sat down and made a list of when it comes up, you know? It’s not like I’m at the grocery store thinking, “Oh, there’s my boss again,” or something.\n\nAlthough grocery stores are weirdly stressful anyway—like the self-checkout yelling at you to put the item in the bagging area when it’s already there. But, sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure if I’d say it “haunts” me. That sounds kind of dramatic. It bothered me, yeah, but I don’t know how much it’s following me around when I’m not thinking about work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares or flashbacks about what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them flashbacks. Like in movies where someone is suddenly back there? I don’t think it’s like that.\n\nNightmares… I mean, I remember waking up tense a couple times, but I couldn’t tell you what I was dreaming about. Sometimes I have weird dreams anyway, like being late for a shift at a job I haven’t had since college, or my teeth falling out—apparently everyone has that one. So I don’t really know if it’s connected to him.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed clear nightmares or flashbacks about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding people, places, or things that remind you of what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way, like avoiding “people, places, or things.” I mean, I avoid the break room sometimes, but that might just be because someone keeps microwaving fish in there, which should honestly be illegal in an office.\n\nBut as far as things that remind me of him or what happened… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m deliberately dodging stuff, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding your boss or situations where you might interact with him?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding him exactly. I mean, I’m not like hiding behind plants when he walks by—though our office plants are mostly fake, so that wouldn’t even work.\n\nI guess if I know he’s going to be in a meeting, I might feel a little… I don’t know, aware of it? But I can’t really say I’ve been planning my day around not seeing him or anything. I still do what I have to do. It’s just awkward, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t sit there thinking, “Don’t think about it,” if that makes sense. Sometimes my mind goes to it and then I get distracted by something else, like an email comes in or someone asks me about a client thing, and then I’m on to that.\n\nIt’s kind of like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there—actually that’s been happening to me forever, not because of this, I think. My mom does it too, so maybe it’s genetic or just too much coffee.\n\nBut yeah, I haven’t really noticed myself trying to push it away on purpose. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what happened with your boss when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, it pops into my head sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s in that “I don’t want to think about this” way, you know?\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally normal, checking a spreadsheet or trying to remember if I paid my electric bill—which, actually, I need to check that because they changed the website and now it’s weirdly hard to find the login—and then maybe I think about work for a second. But I can’t say I’ve noticed it taking over or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… maybe? I’m not totally sure what counts as “remembering it” versus just, like, knowing it happened. Sometimes something at work reminds me of it for a second, but then I’m thinking about five other things, like whether a client called back or why the printer is making that grinding noise again.

So I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it jumping into my head a lot when I’m not trying to think about it. It’s more kind of… there, I guess, but not something I’ve been keeping track of.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what made me finally call the EAP is I’ve been getting really jumpy at work. Like, every little bang in the office—somebody slams a file drawer, or drops a coffee mug—and I jump like it’s this huge thing.  \n\nAnd then my chest keeps racing way longer than it should. It’s embarrassing, honestly. The office isn’t even that loud most of the time, except people are always doing that thing where they yank drawers shut like they’re trying to prove a point… but yeah, that’s the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel jumpy or experience a racing chest in situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Mostly I’ve noticed it at work, yeah. Like the file drawers, or someone drops a mug, and I jump way harder than I should, then my chest keeps going like I ran up stairs or something.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, I guess I’m not around as many sudden bangs, unless you count my neighbor upstairs who walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight—but I don’t know, the thing that really made me call was the office stuff. It just feels ridiculous to be startled by a drawer, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not around any loud noises or sudden movements?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Even when it’s not loud, like when I’m just sitting at my desk, I catch myself checking where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s walking up behind me. Which is stupid because I’m supposed to be watching my team and answering emails and all that.\n\nIt’s not like the office is some dangerous place—it’s mostly people complaining about spreadsheets and the break room coffee—but my attention keeps going there anyway, like I need to know where everybody is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious at home or when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I don’t know. I haven’t really paid as much attention to it there. I’m usually just trying to make dinner or crash on the couch, and then I get distracted by dumb stuff like whether I remembered to take the laundry out—my building’s machines are always full, so if you miss the timing, someone moves your clothes, which is… annoying.\n\nBut anxiety-wise, alone? I guess I’m not sure how to answer that. It’s not as obvious to me as it is at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around other people outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, outside of work I’m usually around people in pretty normal ways—grocery store, maybe grabbing food, my friend had this barbecue thing last weekend but I got there late because parking near his place is a nightmare, there’s this one street where everyone double-parks and then you’re stuck doing a three-point turn forever…\n\nSorry, what were you asking—around other people? I don’t think I’ve noticed anything clear like that. Not in the same way, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet place by yourself?\n\nPatient: Quiet by myself… I don’t know. I mean, quiet is kind of rare, honestly—my apartment has this radiator that clicks even when it’s not doing anything, and the guy upstairs walks like he’s moving furniture at midnight.\n\nBut if you mean like actually alone with no noise… I haven’t really noticed. Sometimes I just put on a show or scroll through stuff, so I’m not sitting there thinking about it. I don’t think I can give you a clear yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're about to go to work or anticipating a workday?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, not always on the way there exactly—like if I’m stuck in traffic I’m mostly mad about traffic, and there’s this one light that takes forever near the office park.\n\nBut once I’m thinking about being in the office, or when I get there, I catch myself… checking stuff. Like where my boss is, where the exits are, whether someone’s coming up behind me. Even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team, I’m sort of scanning the room instead. It’s hard to just focus like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your work or job that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I think it’s tied to that thing with my boss. Or—well, not even the job itself, because the spreadsheets and calls are just normal boring stuff, and I used to complain more about the coffee machine than anything else.\n\nBut when I try to explain what happened, I can remember him slamming the chair and standing in front of the door… like, I can picture that really clearly. But the worst part, the actual words he said to me, it’s just blank. Like that section got cut out. And then being back there makes me feel like I’m trying to fill in something I can’t quite get to.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific and upsetting happen with your boss that might be connected to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… something happened. It was with my boss, in his office—or near his office, I guess. I keep thinking about this stupid detail, like the chair, because I remember him slamming it really hard. And he was standing in front of the door, like right there, so I couldn’t just leave.\n\nBut when I try to say the worst part out loud, like what he actually said to me, it’s… blank. I can’t get the words. It’s weird, because I remember other random stuff from that day, like the carpet pattern and the buzzing light, but the actual part that upset me most is just missing, like somebody cut that section out.\n\nDoctor: Did your boss say or do something that felt threatening or traumatic to you during that incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. The part I can actually remember is him slamming the chair, really hard, and then standing in front of the door like I couldn’t get past him.\n\nBut the actual worst part—what he said to me—I can’t… I don’t have it. It’s like when you’re trying to remember a song lyric and you get every dumb little thing around it instead. I remember the room and the chair and where he was standing, but the words are just blank. Like that section got cut out.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were in danger during the incident with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if “danger” is the right word. I mean, I wasn’t thinking in a neat sentence like that. I just remember the chair thing and him being by the door, and my brain kind of went… like, okay, where do I stand, what do I do with my hands, can I get out without making it worse.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but I keep comparing it to when someone gets too close in an elevator and you suddenly notice every floor number. So maybe I felt trapped more than anything. I’m not totally sure how to answer it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the incident with your boss was something that you couldn't control or that you were powerless to stop?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I guess I didn’t feel like I was in charge of the situation, if that’s what you mean. But “powerless” feels like a big word.\n\nI kept thinking about small stuff, like where to put my eyes, whether moving toward the door would make him more angry. It’s strange, because I manage people at work too, and I’m used to handling tense conversations—like, I’ve had sales guys get really heated over quotas and I can usually talk them down. But in that moment it wasn’t like that. I wasn’t really choosing what happened next, I was just kind of… waiting for it to be over, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the incident with your boss is something that still bothers you or haunts you when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, outside of work… I haven’t really sat down and made a list of when it comes up, you know? It’s not like I’m at the grocery store thinking, “Oh, there’s my boss again,” or something.\n\nAlthough grocery stores are weirdly stressful anyway—like the self-checkout yelling at you to put the item in the bagging area when it’s already there. But, sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure if I’d say it “haunts” me. That sounds kind of dramatic. It bothered me, yeah, but I don’t know how much it’s following me around when I’m not thinking about work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares or flashbacks about what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them flashbacks. Like in movies where someone is suddenly back there? I don’t think it’s like that.\n\nNightmares… I mean, I remember waking up tense a couple times, but I couldn’t tell you what I was dreaming about. Sometimes I have weird dreams anyway, like being late for a shift at a job I haven’t had since college, or my teeth falling out—apparently everyone has that one. So I don’t really know if it’s connected to him.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed clear nightmares or flashbacks about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding people, places, or things that remind you of what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way, like avoiding “people, places, or things.” I mean, I avoid the break room sometimes, but that might just be because someone keeps microwaving fish in there, which should honestly be illegal in an office.\n\nBut as far as things that remind me of him or what happened… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m deliberately dodging stuff, at least not in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding your boss or situations where you might interact with him?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m avoiding him exactly. I mean, I’m not like hiding behind plants when he walks by—though our office plants are mostly fake, so that wouldn’t even work.\n\nI guess if I know he’s going to be in a meeting, I might feel a little… I don’t know, aware of it? But I can’t really say I’ve been planning my day around not seeing him or anything. I still do what I have to do. It’s just awkward, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t sit there thinking, “Don’t think about it,” if that makes sense. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what happened with your boss when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself remembering the incident with your boss when you're not trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? Sometimes something at work reminds me of it for a second, but then I’m thinking about five other things, like whether a client called back or why the printer is making that grinding noise again.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it jumping into my head a lot when I’m not trying to think about it. It’s more kind of… there, I guess, but not something I’ve been keeping track of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, I’m not like hiding behind plants when he walks by—though our office plants are mostly fake, so that wouldn’t even work.\n\nI guess if I know he’s going to be in a meeting, I might feel a little… I don’t know, aware of it? But I can’t really say I’ve been planning my day around not seeing him or anything. I still do what I have to do. It’s just awkward, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened with your boss?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t sit there thinking, “Don’t think about it,” if that makes sense. Sometimes my mind goes to it and then I get distracted by something else, like an email comes in or someone asks me about a client thing, and then I’m on to that.\n\nIt’s kind of like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there—actually that’s been happening to me forever, not because of this, I think. My mom does it too, so maybe it’s genetic or just too much coffee.\n\nBut yeah, I haven’t really noticed myself trying to push it away on purpose. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what happened with your boss when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, it pops into my head sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s in that “I don’t want to think about this” way, you know?\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally normal, checking a spreadsheet or trying to remember if I paid my electric bill—which, actually, I need to check that because they changed the website and now it’s weirdly hard to find the login—and then maybe I think about work for a second. But I can’t say I’ve noticed it taking over or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself remembering the incident with your boss when you're not trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’m not totally sure what counts as “remembering it” versus just, like, knowing it happened. Sometimes something at work reminds me of it for a second, but then I’m thinking about five other things, like whether a client called back or why the printer is making that grinding noise again.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it jumping into my head a lot when I’m not trying to think about it. It’s more kind of… there, I guess, but not something I’ve been keeping track of."
